========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… what finally made me come in is that I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired or in a rut, but it feels bigger than that. I used to make time for things without even thinking about it—painting, hiking on weekends when I could, choir practice, Sunday gatherings with my family—and those were the things that sort of made me feel like me.

Now, even if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could get out my paints,” or “I should call and see what everyone’s doing Sunday,” and I just… don’t care enough to move. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the feeling I used to get from them isn’t there, and I can’t seem to make myself want them. That’s been really unsettling. I suppose that’s why I’m here.

It’s been… about eight months, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, which is part of why I kept telling myself it was just stress or being busy. I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.

But over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.

So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / teaching_assistant / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"What finally brought me in is that I don't feel like myself anymore—I used to make time for painting, hiking, choir, and Sunday family gatherings, and now even when I have a free hour I just sit there unable to care about any of it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.

Like, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.

So maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.

Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.

So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.

I suppose that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this loss of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, which is part of why I kept telling myself it was just stress or being busy. It started building after I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at work. At first I thought, well, of course I’m drained, it’s a big project, there are a lot of moving pieces, and I was putting so much of myself into making it work.\n\nBut then months kept passing, and I realized it wasn’t just that I didn’t have time for painting or hiking or family things. Even when I did have time, I didn’t feel drawn to them anymore. So… yes, I’d say around eight months. That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.\n\nIt’s been building pretty steadily since I took over that STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. There may have been a day here or there where I thought, “Okay, maybe I’m doing a little better,” or I got through things more smoothly, but not a real sustained period. Not two months of feeling back to normal. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time, and if anything it’s just become more noticeable as the months have gone on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.

It’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.

So… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. It feels less like nervous worrying and more like being weighed down, if that makes sense.

I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. No, not for two years. Before this, I had normal ups and downs, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I was down more often than not for years.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… definitely. My sleep has been really erratic for months now. Some nights I get into bed exhausted, and then I just lie there awake, going over little details in my head—especially things connected to the grant and the curriculum work, like what I forgot to follow up on or whether something’s going to fall apart.

And then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s that awful kind of waking up where I’m immediately alert, but not rested—just lying there staring at the ceiling, thinking, “Please, just go back to sleep,” and of course that makes it worse.

There are nights where I technically sleep longer, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I get up feeling like I never really rested at all, like I’ve been awake in some part of my brain the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I almost don’t remember what it feels like to wake up refreshed.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is that I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired or in a rut, but it feels bigger than that. I used to make time for things without even thinking about it—painting, hiking on weekends when I could, choir practice, Sunday gatherings with my family—and those were the things that sort of made me feel like me.\n\nNow, even if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could get out my paints,” or “I should call and see what everyone’s doing Sunday,” and I just… don’t care enough to move. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the feeling I used to get from them isn’t there, and I can’t seem to make myself want them. That’s been really unsettling. I suppose that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this loss of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, which is part of why I kept telling myself it was just stress or being busy. It started building after I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at work. At first I thought, well, of course I’m drained, it’s a big project, there are a lot of moving pieces, and I was putting so much of myself into making it work.\n\nBut then months kept passing, and I realized it wasn’t just that I didn’t have time for painting or hiking or family things. Even when I did have time, I didn’t feel drawn to them anymore. So… yes, I’d say around eight months. That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. I wish I could say I’m still managing the same as before, but I’m really not.

At work it’s been the most obvious. I’ve missed several teaching sessions, which is not like me. I used to be very dependable about showing up, even if I was tired or had a lot going on, and now there have been times where I just couldn’t get myself there or couldn’t face being in front of the students. And I’ve been avoiding some of the student workshops too, partly because they require so much preparation and energy and follow-through, and I just don’t feel like I can keep up with all the responsibilities the way I used to. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know people are relying on me.

At home, my husband has had to step in more. Not in some dramatic way every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable. I’ll pull back from family plans or say I can’t go, or I’ll just sort of disappear into myself and leave him to handle things. Sometimes I don’t feel safe pushing through on my own, if that makes sense—not physically unsafe exactly, but like I don’t trust myself to keep functioning and smiling and pretending I’m okay. So yes, it’s affecting work, home, and how I am with people.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is that I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired or in a rut, but it feels bigger than that. I used to make time for things without even thinking about it—painting, hiking on weekends when I could, choir practice, Sunday gatherings with my family—and those were the things that sort of made me feel like me.\n\nNow, even if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could get out my paints,” or “I should call and see what everyone’s doing Sunday,” and I just… don’t care enough to move. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the feeling I used to get from them isn’t there, and I can’t seem to make myself want them. That’s been really unsettling. I suppose that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this loss of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, which is part of why I kept telling myself it was just stress or being busy. It started building after I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at work. At first I thought, well, of course I’m drained, it’s a big project, there are a lot of moving pieces, and I was putting so much of myself into making it work.\n\nBut then months kept passing, and I realized it wasn’t just that I didn’t have time for painting or hiking or family things. Even when I did have time, I didn’t feel drawn to them anymore. So… yes, I’d say around eight months. That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.\n\nIt’s been building pretty steadily since I took over that STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. There may have been a day here or there where I thought, “Okay, maybe I’m doing a little better,” or I got through things more smoothly, but not a real sustained period. Not two months of feeling back to normal. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time, and if anything it’s just become more noticeable as the months have gone on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. It feels less like nervous worrying and more like being weighed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, had you already been feeling down or depressed more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not for two years. So… no, I wouldn’t say this was already going on for two years before that. It’s more this eight-month stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. My sleep has been really erratic for months now. Some nights I get into bed exhausted, and then I just lie there awake, going over little details in my head—especially things connected to the grant and the curriculum work, like what I forgot to follow up on or whether something’s going to fall apart.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s that awful kind of waking up where I’m immediately alert, but not rested—just lying there staring at the ceiling, thinking, “Please, just go back to sleep,” and of course that makes it worse.\n\nThere are nights where I technically sleep longer, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I get up feeling like I never really rested at all, like I’ve been awake in some part of my brain the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I almost don’t remember what it feels like to wake up refreshed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to function at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say I’m still managing the same as before, but I’m really not.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I’ve missed several teaching sessions, which is not like me. I used to be very dependable about showing up, even if I was tired or had a lot going on, and now there have been times where I just couldn’t get myself there or couldn’t face being in front of the students. And I’ve been avoiding some of the student workshops too, partly because they require so much preparation and energy and follow-through, and I just don’t feel like I can keep up with all the responsibilities the way I used to. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know people are relying on me.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to step in more. Not in some dramatic way every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable. I’ll pull back from family plans or say I can’t go, or I’ll just sort of disappear into myself and leave him to handle things. Sometimes I don’t feel safe pushing through on my own, if that makes sense—not physically unsafe exactly, but like I don’t trust myself to keep functioning and smiling and pretending I’m okay. So yes, it’s affecting work, home, and how I am with people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. At first I thought, well, of course I’m drained, it’s a big project, there are a lot of moving pieces, and I was putting so much of myself into making it work.\n\nBut then months kept passing, and I realized it wasn’t just that I didn’t have time for painting or hiking or family things. Even when I did have time, I didn’t feel drawn to them anymore. So… yes, I’d say around eight months. That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.\n\nIt’s been building pretty steadily since I took over that STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. There may have been a day here or there where I thought, “Okay, maybe I’m doing a little better,” or I got through things more smoothly, but not a real sustained period. Not two months of feeling back to normal. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time, and if anything it’s just become more noticeable as the months have gone on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. It feels less like nervous worrying and more like being weighed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, had you already been feeling down or depressed more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not for two years. Before this, I had normal ups and downs, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I was down more often than not for years.\n\nThis really feels like it started building about eight months ago, around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. At first I think I told myself it was just stress or adjustment, like I just needed to get used to the new responsibilities, but over time it became more constant. So… no, I wouldn’t say this was already going on for two years before that. It’s more this eight-month stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. My sleep has been really erratic for months now. Some nights I get into bed exhausted, and then I just lie there awake, going over little details in my head—especially things connected to the grant and the curriculum work, like what I forgot to follow up on or whether something’s going to fall apart.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s that awful kind of waking up where I’m immediately alert, but not rested—just lying there staring at the ceiling, thinking, “Please, just go back to sleep,” and of course that makes it worse.\n\nThere are nights where I technically sleep longer, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I get up feeling like I never really rested at all, like I’ve been awake in some part of my brain the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I almost don’t remember what it feels like to wake up refreshed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to function at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say I’m still managing the same as before, but I’m really not.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I’ve missed several teaching sessions, which is not like me. I used to be very dependable about showing up, even if I was tired or had a lot going on, and now there have been times where I just couldn’t get myself there or couldn’t face being in front of the students. And I’ve been avoiding some of the student workshops too, partly because they require so much preparation and energy and follow-through, and I just don’t feel like I can keep up with all the responsibilities the way I used to. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know people are relying on me.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to step in more. Not in some dramatic way every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable. I’ll pull back from family plans or say I can’t go, or I’ll just sort of disappear into myself and leave him to handle things. Sometimes I don’t feel safe pushing through on my own, if that makes sense—not physically unsafe exactly, but like I don’t trust myself to keep functioning and smiling and pretending I’m okay. So yes, it’s affecting work, home, and how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Did the work initiative start within three months before these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… or at least it was right around then. The symptoms really started building after I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, and that’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt wasn’t like one exact day where I can say, “That’s when everything changed,” but the timing feels connected. I took on the initiative, and then over the weeks after that I started feeling like I was slipping more and more. So yes, it began within that kind of time frame after the work initiative started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes… I have. Not like I’ve sat down and made a plan or anything like that, but I have had thoughts that really scare me.

It tends to happen late at night, especially when I’m alone in the studio and everything is quiet and I’m already exhausted. I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I almost feel shocked at myself, like, “Why am I thinking that?” because I don’t want to be someone who thinks that way. But it comes up, and it feels very dark and very heavy.

I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. But the thoughts were frightening enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling them by myself anymore. I didn’t want to be alone with them and pretend they weren’t happening.

That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.\n\nIt’s been building pretty steadily since I took over that STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. There may have been a day here or there where I thought, “Okay, maybe I’m doing a little better,” or I got through things more smoothly, but not a real sustained period. Not two months of feeling back to normal. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time, and if anything it’s just become more noticeable as the months have gone on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. It feels less like nervous worrying and more like being weighed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, had you already been feeling down or depressed more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not for two years. Before this, I had normal ups and downs, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I was down more often than not for years.\n\nThis really feels like it started building about eight months ago, around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. At first I think I told myself it was just stress or adjustment, like I just needed to get used to the new responsibilities, but over time it became more constant. So… no, I wouldn’t say this was already going on for two years before that. It’s more this eight-month stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. My sleep has been really erratic for months now. Some nights I get into bed exhausted, and then I just lie there awake, going over little details in my head—especially things connected to the grant and the curriculum work, like what I forgot to follow up on or whether something’s going to fall apart.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s that awful kind of waking up where I’m immediately alert, but not rested—just lying there staring at the ceiling, thinking, “Please, just go back to sleep,” and of course that makes it worse.\n\nThere are nights where I technically sleep longer, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I get up feeling like I never really rested at all, like I’ve been awake in some part of my brain the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I almost don’t remember what it feels like to wake up refreshed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to function at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say I’m still managing the same as before, but I’m really not.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I’ve missed several teaching sessions, which is not like me. I used to be very dependable about showing up, even if I was tired or had a lot going on, and now there have been times where I just couldn’t get myself there or couldn’t face being in front of the students. And I’ve been avoiding some of the student workshops too, partly because they require so much preparation and energy and follow-through, and I just don’t feel like I can keep up with all the responsibilities the way I used to. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know people are relying on me.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to step in more. Not in some dramatic way every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable. I’ll pull back from family plans or say I can’t go, or I’ll just sort of disappear into myself and leave him to handle things. Sometimes I don’t feel safe pushing through on my own, if that makes sense—not physically unsafe exactly, but like I don’t trust myself to keep functioning and smiling and pretending I’m okay. So yes, it’s affecting work, home, and how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Did the work initiative start within three months before these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… or at least it was right around then. The symptoms really started building after I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, and that’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt wasn’t like one exact day where I can say, “That’s when everything changed,” but the timing feels connected. I took on the initiative, and then over the weeks after that I started feeling like I was slipping more and more. So yes, it began within that kind of time frame after the work initiative started.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear, dramatic change in my appetite, at least not in the way people describe where they suddenly can’t eat at all or they’re eating constantly.\n\nIt’s more that I haven’t been paying very close attention, honestly. Some days I eat normally, and other days I realize I’ve sort of skipped a meal or just picked at something, but I don’t know if that’s an appetite thing or just being distracted and not having the energy to organize food. I haven’t been tracking my weight or anything like that, so I can’t really say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I’ve sat down and made a plan or anything like that, but I have had thoughts that really scare me.\n\nIt tends to happen late at night, especially when I’m alone in the studio and everything is quiet and I’m already exhausted. I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I almost feel shocked at myself, like, “Why am I thinking that?” because I don’t want to be someone who thinks that way. But it comes up, and it feels very dark and very heavy.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. But the thoughts were frightening enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling them by myself anymore. I didn’t want to be alone with them and pretend they weren’t happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

And then I almost feel shocked at myself, like, “Why am I thinking that?” because I don’t want to be someone who thinks that way. But it comes up, and it feels very dark and very heavy.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. But the thoughts were frightening enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling them by myself anymore. I didn’t want to be alone with them and pretend they weren’t happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling unusually tired or low in energy nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / teaching_assistant / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I do feel worn down a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually tired in a clear medical sense, or whether it’s just life and work and not sleeping well enough or pushing myself too much.

Most days I feel like I’m dragging, but I haven’t really measured it, if that makes sense. Some mornings I can still get up and do what I need to do, but it takes more effort than I think it used to. And then other days I’ll sit there thinking I should be doing something simple, like making lunch or answering an email, and it feels like my body just doesn’t want to start.

So… yes, maybe low energy, but I’m hesitant because I don’t know what counts as “nearly every day” or “unusually.” I just know I haven’t felt like myself, and a lot of things feel heavier than they should.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is that I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired or in a rut, but it feels bigger than that. I used to make time for things without even thinking about it—painting, hiking on weekends when I could, choir practice, Sunday gatherings with my family—and those were the things that sort of made me feel like me.\n\nNow, even if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could get out my paints,” or “I should call and see what everyone’s doing Sunday,” and I just… don’t care enough to move. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the feeling I used to get from them isn’t there, and I can’t seem to make myself want them. That’s been really unsettling. I suppose that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this loss of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, which is part of why I kept telling myself it was just stress or being busy. It started building after I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at work. At first I thought, well, of course I’m drained, it’s a big project, there are a lot of moving pieces, and I was putting so much of myself into making it work.\n\nBut then months kept passing, and I realized it wasn’t just that I didn’t have time for painting or hiking or family things. Even when I did have time, I didn’t feel drawn to them anymore. So… yes, I’d say around eight months. That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.\n\nIt’s been building pretty steadily since I took over that STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. There may have been a day here or there where I thought, “Okay, maybe I’m doing a little better,” or I got through things more smoothly, but not a real sustained period. Not two months of feeling back to normal. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time, and if anything it’s just become more noticeable as the months have gone on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. It feels less like nervous worrying and more like being weighed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, had you already been feeling down or depressed more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not for two years. Before this, I had normal ups and downs, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I was down more often than not for years.\n\nThis really feels like it started building about eight months ago, around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. At first I think I told myself it was just stress or adjustment, like I just needed to get used to the new responsibilities, but over time it became more constant. So… no, I wouldn’t say this was already going on for two years before that. It’s more this eight-month stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. My sleep has been really erratic for months now. Some nights I get into bed exhausted, and then I just lie there awake, going over little details in my head—especially things connected to the grant and the curriculum work, like what I forgot to follow up on or whether something’s going to fall apart.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s that awful kind of waking up where I’m immediately alert, but not rested—just lying there staring at the ceiling, thinking, “Please, just go back to sleep,” and of course that makes it worse.\n\nThere are nights where I technically sleep longer, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I get up feeling like I never really rested at all, like I’ve been awake in some part of my brain the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I almost don’t remember what it feels like to wake up refreshed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to function at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say I’m still managing the same as before, but I’m really not.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I’ve missed several teaching sessions, which is not like me. I used to be very dependable about showing up, even if I was tired or had a lot going on, and now there have been times where I just couldn’t get myself there or couldn’t face being in front of the students. And I’ve been avoiding some of the student workshops too, partly because they require so much preparation and energy and follow-through, and I just don’t feel like I can keep up with all the responsibilities the way I used to. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know people are relying on me.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to step in more. Not in some dramatic way every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable. I’ll pull back from family plans or say I can’t go, or I’ll just sort of disappear into myself and leave him to handle things. Sometimes I don’t feel safe pushing through on my own, if that makes sense—not physically unsafe exactly, but like I don’t trust myself to keep functioning and smiling and pretending I’m okay. So yes, it’s affecting work, home, and how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Did the work initiative start within three months before these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… or at least it was right around then. The symptoms really started building after I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, and that’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt wasn’t like one exact day where I can say, “That’s when everything changed,” but the timing feels connected. I took on the initiative, and then over the weeks after that I started feeling like I was slipping more and more. So yes, it began within that kind of time frame after the work initiative started.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear, dramatic change in my appetite, at least not in the way people describe where they suddenly can’t eat at all or they’re eating constantly.\n\nIt’s more that I haven’t been paying very close attention, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight or anything like that, so I can’t really say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I don’t know if “worthless” is the word I would have chosen right away, but I do get this really intense feeling that I’m failing people.

It comes up a lot with the curriculum project I’ve been working on. If anything goes wrong — even something small, like a section not being clear enough, or I miss something, or there’s a delay — I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it spirals into this much bigger thing, like I’m wasting this opportunity I was given.

My parents sacrificed a lot for me to be able to get an education and have the kind of work where I can contribute to something meaningful. So when I make mistakes, even normal mistakes, I don’t just think, “Oh, I need to fix that.” I think, “You’re wasting what they gave up for you. You’re not doing enough. You’re disappointing everyone.” It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very true.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is that I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired or in a rut, but it feels bigger than that. I used to make time for things without even thinking about it—painting, hiking on weekends when I could, choir practice, Sunday gatherings with my family—and those were the things that sort of made me feel like me.\n\nNow, even if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could get out my paints,” or “I should call and see what everyone’s doing Sunday,” and I just… don’t care enough to move. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the feeling I used to get from them isn’t there, and I can’t seem to make myself want them. That’s been really unsettling. I suppose that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this loss of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, which is part of why I kept telling myself it was just stress or being busy. It started building after I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at work. At first I thought, well, of course I’m drained, it’s a big project, there are a lot of moving pieces, and I was putting so much of myself into making it work.\n\nBut then months kept passing, and I realized it wasn’t just that I didn’t have time for painting or hiking or family things. Even when I did have time, I didn’t feel drawn to them anymore. So… yes, I’d say around eight months. That’s when I can really trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the stress from taking over that curriculum initiative at work?\n\nPatient: I think it started around then, yes… or at least that’s when I can trace it beginning. When I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, it felt like this big turning point. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress. It’s been building for about eight months now, since that initiative began, and I guess I’ve connected the two because that’s when I first noticed myself pulling away from things I normally cared about. So… yes, the work stress seems like where it started, but it’s also continued in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nDoctor: If the stress from that work initiative were removed, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if the work stress went away I’d just sort of come back online, but… I’m not sure that’s true anymore. The thing that scared me enough to come in is that I don’t feel like myself even when I’m not actively dealing with work.\n\nLike, if I get a free hour, I used to automatically reach for something—my paints, or I’d think about going for a hike, or choir, or Sunday with my family. Those things used to feel like they had some pull. Now I’ll sit there and think, “I could do that,” and then I just… don’t. It’s not even always because I’m busy in that moment. It’s more like I can’t care enough to start.\n\nSo maybe the initiative triggered it or made it worse, but if you took it away tomorrow, I’m afraid this blankness would still be there. That’s the part that doesn’t feel like just stress to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything, though sometimes I do feel like I’m right on the edge of it. It’s more like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I’m moving through the day under it.\n\nI can be in the classroom, surrounded by students and noise and all the normal activity, and still feel strangely hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to any of it. And then I’ll go home and be with my husband, and it’s the same thing. I should feel comforted or present, but instead I can feel this sadness sitting in me, and sometimes I’m close to tears for no obvious reason. So… yes, I’d say it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, was there ever a stretch of two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was. I’ve been trying to think back, because eight months is long enough that the weeks kind of blur together, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt mostly like myself again.\n\nIt’s been building pretty steadily since I took over that STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. There may have been a day here or there where I thought, “Okay, maybe I’m doing a little better,” or I got through things more smoothly, but not a real sustained period. Not two months of feeling back to normal. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time, and if anything it’s just become more noticeable as the months have gone on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if it’s worry in the sense of my mind jumping from one catastrophe to another all day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get stuck on how everything feels hard, or whether I’m keeping up, but I’m not sure it’s a bunch of different worries. I don’t really sit there worrying about every possible thing—money, health, family, the house, all of that—at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nSo… maybe some worry, yes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed excessive worrying about lots of different things. It feels less like nervous worrying and more like being weighed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, had you already been feeling down or depressed more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not for two years. Before this, I had normal ups and downs, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I was down more often than not for years.\n\nThis really feels like it started building about eight months ago, around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. At first I think I told myself it was just stress or adjustment, like I just needed to get used to the new responsibilities, but over time it became more constant. So… no, I wouldn’t say this was already going on for two years before that. It’s more this eight-month stretch that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. My sleep has been really erratic for months now. Some nights I get into bed exhausted, and then I just lie there awake, going over little details in my head—especially things connected to the grant and the curriculum work, like what I forgot to follow up on or whether something’s going to fall apart.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s that awful kind of waking up where I’m immediately alert, but not rested—just lying there staring at the ceiling, thinking, “Please, just go back to sleep,” and of course that makes it worse.\n\nThere are nights where I technically sleep longer, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I get up feeling like I never really rested at all, like I’ve been awake in some part of my brain the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I almost don’t remember what it feels like to wake up refreshed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to function at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say I’m still managing the same as before, but I’m really not.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I’ve missed several teaching sessions, which is not like me. I used to be very dependable about showing up, even if I was tired or had a lot going on, and now there have been times where I just couldn’t get myself there or couldn’t face being in front of the students. And I’ve been avoiding some of the student workshops too, partly because they require so much preparation and energy and follow-through, and I just don’t feel like I can keep up with all the responsibilities the way I used to. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know people are relying on me.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to step in more. Not in some dramatic way every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable. I’ll pull back from family plans or say I can’t go, or I’ll just sort of disappear into myself and leave him to handle things. Sometimes I don’t feel safe pushing through on my own, if that makes sense—not physically unsafe exactly, but like I don’t trust myself to keep functioning and smiling and pretending I’m okay. So yes, it’s affecting work, home, and how I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Did the work initiative start within three months before these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… or at least it was right around then. The symptoms really started building after I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, and that’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt wasn’t like one exact day where I can say, “That’s when everything changed,” but the timing feels connected. I took on the initiative, and then over the weeks after that I started feeling like I was slipping more and more. So yes, it began within that kind of time frame after the work initiative started.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear, dramatic change in my appetite, at least not in the way people describe where they suddenly can’t eat at all or they’re eating constantly.\n\nIt’s more that I haven’t been paying very close attention, honestly. Some days I eat normally, and other days I realize I’ve sort of skipped a meal or just picked at something, but I don’t know if that’s an appetite thing or just being distracted and not having the energy to organize food. I haven’t been tracking my weight or anything like that, so I can’t really say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I’ve sat down and made a plan or anything like that, but I have had thoughts that really scare me.\n\nIt tends to happen late at night, especially when I’m alone in the studio and everything is quiet and I’m already exhausted. I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I almost feel shocked at myself, like, “Why am I thinking that?” because I don’t want to be someone who thinks that way. But it comes up, and it feels very dark and very heavy.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. I don’t know if “worthless” is the word I would have chosen right away, but I do get this really intense feeling that I’m failing people.\n\nIt comes up a lot with the curriculum project I’ve been working on. At first I honestly thought, “Okay, this is just a stressful project, I’m stretched thin, anyone would feel worn out.” So I didn’t really question it.\n\nBut over time—maybe because it kept going and didn’t let up—it started to feel like more than ordinary work stress.
